    1: \section{Higher-degree simple-Parry causal obstructions}
    2: \label{sec:simple-parry-causal-obstructions}
    3: 
    4: The equalities between bounded-multiple order and optimal causal inverse
    5: length proved in Theorem~\ref{thm:quadratic-optimal-causal-inverse} do not
    6: extend universally beyond the stated quadratic cases.  We now identify the
    7: finite object that governs
    8: causal inversion in arbitrary simple-Parry rank folds and exhibit
    9: higher-degree counterexamples in both directions.  This section continues to
   10: concern the cyclic language rank of this article, not equality of numerical
   11: $\beta$-values.
   12: 
   13: Recall that Pisot bases have eventually periodic greedy expansions of one
   14: \cite{Schmidt1980}, whereas the finite expansion required below is the
   15: simple-Parry hypothesis.  The collision results through
   16: Theorem~\ref{thm:simple-parry-aperture-two} do not require the base to be
   17: Pisot.  Parry admissibility and the associated Bertrand numeration are standard
   18: \cite{Parry1960,CharlierCisterninoStipulanti2022}; the expansions of one for
   19: cubic Pisot numbers were classified by Bassino \cite{Bassino2002}.  We record
   20: the data needed
   21: to distinguish these published inputs from the new sliding-congruence
   22: statement.
   23: 
   24: Let $\beta\notin\mathbb Z$ be a simple-Parry number with
   25: \[
   26: d_\beta(1)=t_1t_2\cdots t_p0^\infty,
   27: \qquad t_1=d=\lfloor\beta\rfloor,
   28: \qquad t_p>0,
   29: \]
   30: where $p\ge2$ and $0\le t_i\le d$.  Put
   31: \[
   32: P_\beta(z)=z^p-t_1z^{p-1}-\cdots-t_p,
   33: \qquad
   34: \mathcal A_d=\{0,1,\ldots,d\},
   35: \qquad
   36: \Delta_d=[-d,d]\cap\mathbb Z.
   37: \]
   38: The raw system is the full shift $\mathcal A_d^{\mathbb Z}$, so $\Delta_d$
   39: is exactly its digit-difference alphabet.  Let $Q_n$ be the number of legal
   40: length-$n$ words.  In the low-to-high convention used throughout this
   41: article, the established Parry--Bertrand rank data are
   42: \begin{align}
   43: Q_0&=1,\nonumber\\
   44: Q_n&=1+\sum_{i=1}^n t_iQ_{n-i} &&(1\le n<p),\label{eq:simple-parry-Q}\\
   45: Q_n&=\sum_{i=1}^p t_iQ_{n-i} &&(n\ge p),\nonumber
   46: \end{align}
   47: and
   48: \[
   49: \Rank_{\beta,m}(w)=\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}w_jQ_j
   50: \]
   51: maps the legal length-$m$ words, in colexicographic order, bijectively onto
   52: $\{0,\ldots,Q_m-1\}$.  We therefore define $\Fold_{\beta,m}$ and
   53: $\Phi_{\beta,m}$ by the same rank reduction modulo $Q_m$ as in
   54: Definition~\ref{def:quadratic-parry-fold}.
   55: 
   56: For $m\ge2$, define
   57: \begin{equation}
   58: \ell_{\rm cau}(\beta,m)
   59: =\min\{L\ge1:a_t=\psi(y_t,\ldots,y_{t+L-1})
   60: \text{ for every }y=\Phi_{\beta,m}(a)\},
   61: \label{eq:simple-parry-causal-length}
   62: \end{equation}
   63: with value $\infty$ if no such $L$ exists.  Also set
   64: \[
   65: \mathfrak a(P_\beta,d)
   66: =\min\{\deg H:0\ne H\in(P_\beta),\ H(0)\ne0,
   67: \ |[z^j]H|\le d\text{ for all }j\}.
   68: \]
   69: 
   70: \begin{proposition}[Degeneration of the bounded-multiple order]
   71: \label{prop:simple-parry-bounded-order}
   72: Under the preceding simple-Parry hypotheses,
   73: \[
   74: \mathfrak a(P_\beta,d)=p.
   75: \]
   76: \end{proposition}
   77: 
   78: \begin{proof}
   79: The coefficient vector of $P_\beta$ consists of the leading coefficient
   80: $1$ and the coefficients $-t_1,\ldots,-t_p$.  It lies in $\Delta_d$, and its
   81: constant coefficient $-t_p$ is nonzero.  Hence
   82: $\mathfrak a(P_\beta,d)\le p$.  Conversely, every nonzero member of the
   83: principal ideal $(P_\beta)\subset\mathbb Z[z]$ has degree at least
   84: $\deg P_\beta=p$.  This proves equality.
   85: \end{proof}
   86: 
   87: For $L\ge1$, introduce the bounded sliding-congruence obstruction
   88: \begin{equation}
   89: \mathcal N_{\beta,m,L}
   90: =\left\{e\in\Delta_d^{m+L-1}:
   91: e_0\ne0,\quad
   92: \sum_{j=0}^{m-1}Q_je_{t+j}\equiv0\pmod{Q_m}
   93: \ (0\le t<L)\right\}.
   94: \label{eq:bad-sliding-congruence-set}
   95: \end{equation}
   96: Equivalently, let $T_{m,L}$ be the $L$-row banded Toeplitz matrix obtained by
   97: placing the kernel $(Q_0,\ldots,Q_{m-1})$ at offsets
   98: $t=0,\ldots,L-1$.  Then the last condition is
   99: $T_{m,L}e\equiv0\pmod{Q_m}$.
  100: 
  101: \begin{theorem}[Exact causal collision criterion]
  102: \label{thm:simple-parry-causal-collision}
  103: For every nonintegral simple-Parry number as above and every $m\ge2$,
  104: \begin{equation}
  105: \ell_{\rm cau}(\beta,m)
  106: =\min\{L\ge1:\mathcal N_{\beta,m,L}=\varnothing\},
  107: \label{eq:exact-causal-obstruction}
  108: \end{equation}
  109: where the minimum is $\infty$ when all the obstruction sets are nonempty.
  110: 
  111: More explicitly, let $\Gamma_{\beta,m}$ be the directed graph with vertex set
  112: $V=\Delta_d^{m-1}$.  There is an edge from
  113: $v=(v_0,\ldots,v_{m-2})$ to $w=(w_0,\ldots,w_{m-2})$ precisely when
  114: \[
  115: w_i=v_{i+1}\quad(0\le i<m-2)
  116: \]
  117: and
  118: \begin{equation}
  119: \sum_{j=0}^{m-2}Q_jv_j+Q_{m-1}w_{m-2}
  120: \equiv0\pmod{Q_m}.
  121: \label{eq:collision-graph-edge}
  122: \end{equation}
  123: If $B=\{v\in V:v_0\ne0\}$, then
  124: \begin{equation}
  125: \ell_{\rm cau}(\beta,m)
  126: =1+\sup\{\text{number of edges in a directed path starting in }B\}.
  127: \label{eq:collision-graph-longest-path}
  128: \end{equation}
  129: The supremum is infinite exactly when a directed cycle is reachable from
  130: $B$.  Furthermore,
  131: \begin{equation}
  132: \Phi_{\beta,m}\text{ is injective}
  133: \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
  134: \Gamma_{\beta,m}\text{ has no bi-infinite path other than the zero path}.
  135: \label{eq:collision-graph-injectivity}
  136: \end{equation}
  137: If $\Phi_{\beta,m}$ is injective, then its causal inverse is finite exactly
  138: when the zero vertex is not reachable from $B$.
  139: \end{theorem}
  140: 
  141: \begin{proof}
  142: Let $u,v\in\mathcal A_d^{m+L-1}$ and put $e_i=u_i-v_i$.  Since the legal
  143: rank is a bijection, the $t$th output symbols of the two blocks are equal if
  144: and only if their raw ranks agree modulo $Q_m$, namely if and only if
  145: \[
  146: \sum_{j=0}^{m-1}Q_je_{t+j}\equiv0\pmod{Q_m}.
  147: \]
  148: Moreover, $u_0\ne v_0$ if and only if $e_0\ne0$.  Thus every pair of raw
  149: blocks whose first symbols remain ambiguous after $L$ outputs determines an
  150: element of $\mathcal N_{\beta,m,L}$.
  151: 
  152: Conversely, for any $e\in\Delta_d^{m+L-1}$ define coordinatewise
  153: \[
  154: u_i=\max\{e_i,0\},\qquad v_i=\max\{-e_i,0\}.
  155: \]
  156: Then $u_i,v_i\in\mathcal A_d$, $u_i-v_i=e_i$, and both finite blocks extend
  157: arbitrarily to points of the full shift.  Hence every element of
  158: $\mathcal N_{\beta,m,L}$ is realized by an actual output collision with
  159: distinct first symbols.  If the obstruction set is empty, the first raw
  160: symbol is constant on every fiber over an occurring length-$L$ output word;
  161: the resulting function on this finite output language is the decoder $\psi$
  162: in \eqref{eq:simple-parry-causal-length}.  This proves
  163: \eqref{eq:exact-causal-obstruction}.
  164: 
  165: A length-$L$ path in $\Gamma_{\beta,m}$ beginning at
  166: $(e_0,\ldots,e_{m-2})\in B$ is exactly a vector
  167: $e\in\mathcal N_{\beta,m,L}$: each edge appends one coordinate and imposes
  168: one of the $L$ congruences.  Formula
  169: \eqref{eq:collision-graph-longest-path} follows.  Since the graph is finite,
  170: arbitrarily long such paths exist exactly when a directed cycle is reachable
  171: from $B$.
  172: 
  173: A bi-infinite difference sequence labels a bi-infinite path in the graph if
  174: and only if all corresponding output ranks agree.  Every coordinate of that
  175: difference sequence is realized by two raw digits by the positive--negative
  176: part construction above.  Therefore a nonzero bi-infinite graph path is
  177: equivalent to a pair of distinct points with the same image, proving
  178: \eqref{eq:collision-graph-injectivity}.
  179: 
  180: Finally assume injectivity.  No directed cycle containing a nonzero vertex
  181: can then occur, because its periodic repetition would be a nonzero
  182: bi-infinite path.  The zero loop is consequently the only cycle reachable
  183: from $B$ that is compatible with injectivity.  Thus paths from $B$ have
  184: unbounded length exactly when they reach the zero vertex.  Combining this
  185: with \eqref{eq:collision-graph-longest-path} proves the last assertion.
  186: \end{proof}
  187: 
  188: \begin{corollary}[Causal completeness and periodic detection]
  189: \label{cor:simple-parry-causal-completeness}
  190: Under the preceding hypotheses, for every $m\ge2$ the following are
  191: equivalent:
  192: \begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)}]
  193:   \item $\Phi_{\beta,m}$ is injective;
  194:   \item $\ell_{\rm cau}(\beta,m)<\infty$;
  195:   \item no directed cycle of $\Gamma_{\beta,m}$ is reachable from $B$.
  196: \end{enumerate}
  197: If these conditions hold, then
  198: \begin{equation}
  199: 1\le \ell_{\rm cau}(\beta,m)
  200: \le (2d+1)^{m-1}-1.
  201: \label{eq:simple-parry-causal-state-bound}
  202: \end{equation}
  203: If they fail, there are distinct periodic raw points with the same image and
  204: with common period at most $(2d+1)^{m-1}-1$.  Thus injectivity in this class
  205: can be decided by a finite forward cycle search, and every failure has a
  206: bounded periodic certificate.
  207: \end{corollary}
  208: 
  209: \begin{proof}
  210: The recurrence \eqref{eq:simple-parry-Q} gives $Q_1=d+1$ and shows
  211: inductively that $Q_n>Q_{n-1}$: before the terminal index there is the
  212: additional summand $1$, while at and after that index the positive term
  213: $t_pQ_{n-p}$ accompanies $dQ_{n-1}$.  In particular, $Q_m>d$.
  214: 
  215: Let $\mathbf0=(0,\ldots,0)\in\Delta_d^{m-1}$.  If an edge
  216: $v\to\mathbf0$ exists, the overlap conditions give
  217: $v_1=\cdots=v_{m-2}=0$, and the edge congruence reduces to
  218: $v_0\equiv0\pmod{Q_m}$.  Since $|v_0|\le d<Q_m$, necessarily
  219: $v=\mathbf0$.  Hence the zero vertex has the zero vertex as its unique
  220: predecessor and is not reachable from $B$.
  221: 
  222: If $\Phi_{\beta,m}$ is injective, the last assertion of
  223: Theorem~\ref{thm:simple-parry-causal-collision} now gives a finite causal
  224: inverse.  The converse is immediate because a decoder recovers every raw
  225: coordinate.  A directed cycle reachable from $B$ cannot contain
  226: $\mathbf0$; repeating its first-coordinate labels gives a nonzero periodic
  227: difference sequence and hence, by taking positive and negative parts, two
  228: distinct periodic raw points with the same image.  Conversely, from any
  229: nonzero bi-infinite collision path, shift to a vertex in $B$.  Its forward
  230: path avoids $\mathbf0$ and therefore repeats among the
  231: $(2d+1)^{m-1}-1$ nonzero vertices, producing a reachable directed cycle.
  232: This proves all three equivalences and the periodic certificate, whose cycle
  233: may be chosen simple.
  234: 
  235: In the injective case the forward subgraph reachable from $B$ is acyclic and
  236: contains at most $(2d+1)^{m-1}-1$ vertices.  A directed path in it has at
  237: most $(2d+1)^{m-1}-2$ edges.  Formula
  238: \eqref{eq:collision-graph-longest-path} then gives
  239: \eqref{eq:simple-parry-causal-state-bound}.
  240: \end{proof}
  241: 
  242: \begin{theorem}[Complete aperture-two trichotomy]
  243: \label{thm:simple-parry-aperture-two}
  244: Set
  245: \[
  246: c_\beta=t_2+\ind_{\{p\ge3\}}.
  247: \]
  248: Then $1\le c_\beta\le d+1$ and
  249: \[
  250: Q_2=d(d+1)+c_\beta.
  251: \]
  252: Exactly one of the following alternatives holds.
  253: \begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\alph*)}]
  254:   \item If $c_\beta=d+1$, then $\Fold_{\beta,2}$ is the identity on
  255:   $\mathcal A_d^2$ and $\ell_{\rm cau}(\beta,2)=1$.
  256:   \item If $1\le c_\beta<d$, then $\Phi_{\beta,2}$ is injective and
  257:   $\ell_{\rm cau}(\beta,2)=2$.
  258:   \item If $c_\beta=d$, then $\Phi_{\beta,2}$ is noninjective.  Its sole
  259:   nonsingleton path-space fiber is
  260:   \[
  261:   \Phi_{\beta,2}(0^{\mathbb Z})
  262:   =\Phi_{\beta,2}(d^{\mathbb Z})
  263:   =((0,0))^{\mathbb Z},
  264:   \]
  265:   and $\ell_{\rm cau}(\beta,2)=\infty$.
  266: \end{enumerate}
  267: For $p=2$ the three cases are determined by $c_\beta=t_2$; for
  268: $p\ge3$ they are determined by $c_\beta=t_2+1$.  In particular, a binary
  269: simple-Parry word of order at least three has an identity aperture-two fold
  270: when $t_2=1$ and exactly the displayed constant branch pair when $t_2=0$.
  271: \end{theorem}
  272: 
  273: \begin{proof}
  274: Put $q=d+1$ and $M=Q_2=dq+c_\beta$.  The formula for $Q_2$ follows directly
  275: from \eqref{eq:simple-parry-Q}, with the boundary summand $1$ present exactly
  276: when $2<p$.  Since $t_2\ge1$ when $p=2$, whereas $0\le t_2\le d$ when
  277: $p\ge3$, the stated range for $c_\beta$ follows.
  278: 
  279: An edge of $\Gamma_{\beta,2}$ is a pair $(e,f)\in\Delta_d^2$ satisfying
  280: \begin{equation}
  281: e+qf\equiv0\pmod M.
  282: \label{eq:aperture-two-edge-congruence}
  283: \end{equation}
  284: The left side has modulus at most
  285: $d(1+q)=q^2-1<2M$, so it equals $-M$, $0$, or $M$.  If it is zero, then
  286: $q>|e|$ forces $(e,f)=(0,0)$.  If it is $M$, the bound $e\le d$ forces
  287: $f=d$, because $f\le d-1$ would give
  288: $M-qf\ge q+c_\beta>d$; hence $(e,f)=(c_\beta,d)$, which is admissible
  289: exactly when $c_\beta\le d$.  The case $-M$ is its negative.  Thus the full
  290: edge set is
  291: \[
  292: \{(0,0)\}\quad(c_\beta=d+1),
  293: \qquad
  294: \{(0,0),\ \pm(c_\beta,d)\}\quad(c_\beta\le d).
  295: \]
  296: 
  297: When $c_\beta=d+1$, one has $Q_2=q^2=|\mathcal A_d^2|$, so every raw
  298: length-two word is legal and cyclic reduction does nothing.  This proves
  299: (a).  If $c_\beta<d$, the two nonzero edges cannot be followed by another
  300: edge, because their terminal vertices are $\pm d$, not $\pm c_\beta$.
  301: There is a one-output collision but no two-output collision, so
  302: Theorem~\ref{thm:simple-parry-causal-collision} gives (b).  Finally, when
  303: $c_\beta=d$, the two nonzero edges are the loops $(d,d)$ and $(-d,-d)$.
  304: They give precisely the constant difference sequences $\pm d^{\mathbb Z}$.
  305: The only raw realization of either is the pair
  306: $\{0^{\mathbb Z},d^{\mathbb Z}\}$, and the common residue is zero in every
  307: window.  No other nonzero bi-infinite path exists, proving (c) and the fiber
  308: classification.
  309: \end{proof}
  310: 
  311: \begin{proposition}[Pisot-free scope is strict]
  312: \label{prop:nonpisot-simple-parry-scope}
  313: Proposition~\ref{prop:simple-parry-bounded-order},
  314: Theorem~\ref{thm:simple-parry-causal-collision}, Corollary
  315: \ref{cor:simple-parry-causal-completeness}, and Theorem
  316: \ref{thm:simple-parry-aperture-two} hold for every nonintegral simple-Parry
  317: number; no Pisot hypothesis is required.  This strictly enlarges their scope.
  318: Indeed, let $\beta$ be the positive root of
  319: \begin{equation}
  320: x^4-2x^3-2=0.
  321: \label{eq:nonpisot-simple-parry-witness}
  322: \end{equation}
  323: Then
  324: \[
  325: d_\beta(1)=2002\,0^\infty,
  326: \]
  327: $\beta$ is not Pisot, and the aperture-two cyclic rank fold is injective with
  328: $\ell_{\rm cau}(\beta,2)=2$.
  329: \end{proposition}

  227: \subsection{The bounded sliding-congruence lemma}
  228: 
  229: The separation estimate used in the sliding argument is isolated first.  In
  230: particular, the integer multiple and the error term are both retained.
  231: 
  232: \begin{lemma}[Nearest-multiple separation]
  233: \label{lem:nearest-multiple-separation}
  234: Let $Q_0,Q_1,\ldots$ satisfy either \eqref{eq:qminus} or \eqref{eq:qplus},
  235: write $s=1$ or $s=-1$ accordingly, and put
  236: \[
  237: d=\begin{cases}a,&s=1,\\a-1,&s=-1.\end{cases}
  238: \]
  239: Then, for $r\ge4$ and $1\le |e|\le d$,
  240: \begin{equation}
  241: \operatorname{dist}(beQ_{r-1},Q_r\mathbb Z)\ge Q_{r-2}.
  242: \label{eq:quadratic-separation}
  243: \end{equation}
  244: \end{lemma}
  245: 
  246: \begin{proof}
  247: Distance is unchanged by replacing $e$ with $-e$, so assume $1\le e\le d$.
  248: Set
  249: \[
  250: R_r:=\frac{Q_{r-1}}{Q_{r-2}}.
  251: \]
  252: The recurrence gives $R_{r+1}=a+sb/R_r$.  Its initial values and the parameter
  253: ranges imply, by induction,
  254: \begin{align}
  255: a+\frac{b}{a+1}&\le R_r<a+\frac ba &&(s=1,\ r\ge3),
  256: \label{eq:ratio-plus}\\
  257: a-\frac{b}{a-1}&<R_r\le a-\frac ba &&(s=-1,\ r\ge3).
  258: \label{eq:ratio-minus}
  259: \end{align}
  260: For completeness, in the positive-recurrence case the map
  261: $x\mapsto a+b/x$ reverses order, sends
  262: $[a+b/(a+1),a+b/a)$ into itself, and $R_3=a+b/(a+1)$.
  263: In the negative-recurrence case $x\mapsto a-b/x$ preserves order, the interval
  264: in \eqref{eq:ratio-minus} lies in $(a-1,a)$, and $R_3=a-b/a$.
  265: These observations prove the two inductions, including the stated strict
  266: endpoints.
  267: 
  268: We shall also use the following sharpened bounds, obtained by applying the
  269: same recurrence once more:
  270: \begin{align*}
  271: L_+&:=a+\frac{b}{a+b/(a+b/(a+1))},&
  272: U_+&:=a+\frac{b}{a+b/(a+1)},\\
  273: L_-&:=a-\frac{b}{a-1},&
  274: U_-&:=a-\frac{b}{a-b/a}.
  275: \end{align*}
  276: Thus
  277: \begin{equation}
  278: L_+\le R_r\le U_+\quad(s=1,r\ge4),
  279: \qquad
  280: L_-<R_r\le U_-\quad(s=-1,r\ge4).
  281: \label{eq:sharpened-ratio-bounds}
  282: \end{equation}
  283: The first assertion follows because the order-reversing map interchanges the
  284: endpoints $R_4=U_+$ and $R_5=L_+$ and maps $[L_+,U_+]$ into itself.  The
  285: second follows because $R_3=a-b/a$, $R_4=U_-$, and the increasing map sends
  286: $(L_-,U_-]$ into itself.
  287: 
  288: Choose a nearest integer $k$ to
  289: \[
  290: z:=\frac{beQ_{r-1}}{Q_r}=\frac{be}{R_{r+1}}.
  291: \]
  292: The lower bounds $R_{r+1}>a$ for $s=1$ and $R_{r+1}>a-1$ for $s=-1$ give
  293: \[
  294: 0<z<b.
  295: \]
  296: Indeed $e\le a$ in the first case and $e\le a-1$ in the second.  Hence every
  297: nearest integer satisfies
  298: \begin{equation}
  299: 0\le k\le b.
  300: \label{eq:nearest-k-bound}
  301: \end{equation}
  302: Put $h:=be-ak$.  The exact error, with no omitted remainder, is
  303: \begin{align}
  304: E&:=beQ_{r-1}-kQ_r\notag\\
  305:  &=(be-ak)Q_{r-1}-skbQ_{r-2}
  306:    =(hR_r-skb)Q_{r-2}.
  307: \label{eq:nearest-error}
  308: \end{align}
  309: 
  310: We first record the bounded integer exclusion for the cancelling sign in the
  311: positive recurrence.  For $1\le e\le a$, $0\le k\le b$, and $h=be-ak$,
  312: \begin{equation}
  313: h>0\Longrightarrow
  314:   hL_+-kb\ge1\quad\hbox{or}\quad kb-hU_+\ge1.
  315: \label{eq:endpoint-plus}
  316: \end{equation}
  317: Write $j=kb-ah$.  Since $h=be-ak$, solving for $(e,k)$ gives
  318: \[
  319: k=\frac{ah+j}{b},\qquad
  320: e=\frac{(a^2+b)h+aj}{b^2}.
  321: \]
  322: Thus the two displayed numerators are divisible by $b$ and $b^2$,
  323: respectively.  We give the remaining Euclidean divisions explicitly.  Write
  324: \[
  325: be=aq+r,\qquad qb=ap+t,\qquad 0\le r,t<a.
  326: \]
  327: If $k\le q-1$, then $h\ge a+r$ and
  328: \[
  329: hL_+-kb>(a+r)a-(b-1)b\ge1.
  330: \]
  331: If $k\ge q+1$, then $h<0$, so this case is not in the hypothesis of
  332: \eqref{eq:endpoint-plus}.  Hence only $k=q$ remains, and then $h=r$.
  333: Now $j=a(p-r)+t$.  The cases $p<r$ and $p>r$ give, respectively,
  334: $hL_+-kb\ge1$ and $kb-hU_+\ge1$.  Explicitly, when $p<r$,
  335: \[
  336: rL_+-qb=a(r-p)-t+r(L_+-a)>a-t\ge1,
  337: \]
  338: whereas, when $p>r$, the bounds $0<U_+-a<b/a$ and $r<a$ give
  339: \[
  340: qb-rU_+=a(p-r)+t-r(U_+-a)>a-\frac{rb}{a}\ge1.
  341: \]
  342: When $p=r$, put $w=rb-at$.  The two division identities give
  343: \begin{equation}
  344: b^2e=r(a^2+b)+at,\qquad w=r(a^2+2b)-b^2e.
  345: \label{eq:plus-terminal-divisions}
  346: \end{equation}
  347: The integer $w$ is nonzero.  To verify this without a hidden coprimality
  348: assumption, suppose $w=0$ and write $a=gA$, $b=gB$ with
  349: $\gcd(A,B)=1$.  From $at=rb$ one obtains $r=AR$ and $t=BR$ with
  350: $0<R<g$.  Reducing $be=aq+r$ and $qb=ar+t$ modulo $g$ gives
  351: $g\mid AR$ and $g\mid BR$.  Bezout's identity gives $g\mid R$, a
  352: contradiction.  Substitution of
  353: \eqref{eq:plus-terminal-divisions}, followed by multiplication by the positive
  354: denominators, gives the exhaustive terminal alternatives
  355: \[
  356: \begin{array}{c|c}
  357: w>0& rb(a^2+a+b)\ge(t+1)(a^3+a^2+2ab+b),\\
  358: w<0& t(a^2+a+b)\ge rb(a+1)+(a^2+a+b).
  359: \end{array}
  360: \]
  361: There is no ratio estimate hidden in this table.  Indeed,
  362: \[
  363: U_+-a=\frac{b(a+1)}{a^2+a+b},\qquad
  364: L_+-a=\frac{b(a^2+a+b)}{a^3+a^2+2ab+b}.
  365: \]
  366: Thus the first row is exactly $r(L_+-a)-t\ge1$, after multiplication by
  367: its positive denominator, and the second is exactly
  368: $t-r(U_+-a)\ge1$.  The signs are exhaustive because $w\ne0$; the two
  369: identities in \eqref{eq:plus-terminal-divisions} retain every quotient and
  370: remainder used in clearing the denominators.  This proves
  371: \eqref{eq:endpoint-plus}.
  372: 
  373: The cancelling sign for the negative recurrence has a shorter proof which
  374: avoids Euclidean endpoint bookkeeping.  Let $\beta$ and $\alpha$ be the
  375: roots of $x^2-ax+b$, with $0<\alpha<1<\beta$, and put
  376: $g=-h=ak-be>0$.  The closed formula for the recurrence gives
  377: \[
  378: R_r=\frac{\beta^r-\alpha^r}{\beta^{r-1}-\alpha^{r-1}}>\beta,
  379: \]
  380: while \eqref{eq:sharpened-ratio-bounds} gives $R_r\le U_-$.  Consider the
  381: nonzero integral norm
  382: \[
  383: \nu:=k^2-aek+be^2=(k-e\alpha)(k-e\beta).
  384: \]
  385: Here $k\le b\le a-2<\beta\le e\beta$, so the second factor is negative.
  386: 
  387: If $\nu<0$, then $k-e\alpha>0$, and $b=\alpha\beta$ and
  388: $\beta^2=a\beta-b$ give
  389: \[
  390: g\beta-kb=\beta^2(k-e\alpha)
  391: =\frac{\beta^2|\nu|}{e\beta-k}>1;
  392: \]
  393: the last inequality uses $e\le a-1<\beta$.  Hence $gR_r-kb>1$.
  394: 
  395: Suppose $\nu>0$ and set $\delta=e\alpha-k>0$.  Then
  396: \[
  397: g=e\alpha^2-a\delta>0,
  398: \qquad
  399: \nu=\delta\{e(\beta-\alpha)+\delta\}\ge1.
  400: \]
  401: In particular
  402: $0<\delta<e\alpha^2/a\le(a-1)\alpha^2/a<1$.  Put $D=a^2-b$ and
  403: $H=(a-1)(\beta-\alpha)+1$.  Since $e\le a-1$, the norm identity gives
  404: $\delta>H^{-1}$.  Moreover
  405: \[
  406: \alpha-\frac{ab}{D}=\frac{\alpha^3}{D},
  407: \]
  408: and therefore
  409: \begin{align*}
  410: kb-gU_-
  411: &=\beta^2\delta-\frac{g\alpha^3}{D}\\
  412: &=\delta\left(\beta^2+\frac{a\alpha^3}{D}\right)
  413:   -\frac{e\alpha^5}{D}\\
  414: &>\frac{\beta^2}{H}-\frac{(a-1)\alpha^5}{D}>1.
  415: \end{align*}
  416: For the final inequality, all margins are displayed:
  417: \[
  418: D-H=2\alpha^2+\alpha(\beta-1)+(\beta-1)>0
  419: \]
  420: and
  421: \[
  422: \beta^2-H=\beta-1-\alpha(1-\alpha)>(a-1)\alpha^5.
  423: \]
  424: Indeed $\alpha^5<\alpha$, while $b\le a-2$ is equivalent to
  425: $\beta(1-\alpha)\ge2-\alpha>1$.  Thus
  426: $(\beta^2-H)/H>(a-1)\alpha^5/D$.  Since $R_r\le U_-$, this case gives
  427: $kb-gR_r>1$.
  428: 
  429: We now cover all signs explicitly.  If $s=1$ and $h<0$, then
  430: \[
  431: |hR_r-kb|=(-h)R_r+kb>1.
  432: \]
  433: If $h=0$, then $k\ge1$ (because $e\ge1$), and
  434: $|hR_r-kb|=kb\ge1$.  If $h>0$, the two alternatives in
  435: \eqref{eq:endpoint-plus}, together with
  436: $L_+\le R_r\le U_+$, give $|hR_r-kb|\ge1$.
  437: 
  438: If $s=-1$ and $h>0$, then $hR_r+kb>1$.  If $h=0$, again $k\ge1$ and the
  439: modulus is $kb\ge1$.  The case $h<0$ was proved directly above.
  440: Thus in both chambers
  441: \[
  442: |hR_r-skb|\ge1.
  443: \]
  444: Equation~\eqref{eq:nearest-error} now yields $|E|\ge Q_{r-2}$, which is
  445: \eqref{eq:quadratic-separation}.
  446: \end{proof}

  960: \[
  961: \exp\left(\sum_{n\ge1}\frac{(q^n-1)z^n}{n}\right)
  962: =\frac{1-z}{1-qz}.
  963: \]
  964: 
  965: The displayed graph is right- and left-resolving and follower-separated.
  966: Every vertex is reached by a one-symbol label different from $(0,0)$, which
  967: is intrinsically synchronizing.  Lemma~\ref{lem:fischer-cover-criterion}
  968: therefore identifies it as the right Fischer cover.
  969: 
  970: To see that the image is not an SFT, fix $N$ and choose $L>N$.  The word
  971: \[
  972: (d,0)(0,0)^L(d,0)
  973: \]
  974: is not admissible: the first anchor forces the intervening hidden state to be
  975: $0$, while the second requires it to be $d$.  Every subword of length at
  976: most $N+1$ meets at most one anchor and is admissible, using the constant
  977: $0$ or constant $d$ hidden path as appropriate.  Thus no finite forbidden
  978: list defines the image.
  979: \end{proof}
  980: 
  981: \begin{theorem}[Exact finite-block onset, Fischer cover, and Markov order]
  982: \label{thm:quadratic-exact-local-order}
  983: Let $m\ge m_*(\beta)$ and define
  984: \[
  985: \delta(\beta,m)=\min\{n\ge1:\Phi_{\beta,m,n}\text{ is injective}\}.
  986: \]
  987: Then
  988: \[
  989: \delta(\beta,m)=m.
  990: \]
  991: The natural overlap graph with vertex set
  992: $\mathcal A_\beta^{m-1}$ and edge labels $\Fold_{\beta,m}$ is the right
  993: Fischer cover of $Y_{\beta,m}$ and has exactly
  994: $(\lfloor\beta\rfloor+1)^{m-1}$ vertices.  In the output alphabet fixed in
  995: Definition~\ref{def:quadratic-parry-fold}, the image is an $m$-step SFT but
  996: not an $(m-1)$-step SFT.
  997: \end{theorem}
  998: 
  999: \begin{proof}
 1000: The upper bound $\delta(\beta,m)\le m$ is
 1001: Theorem~\ref{thm:full-quadratic-pisot-threshold}.  For $m\ge3$ the reverse
 1002: inequality has a uniform certificate.  In the negative chamber, place the
 1003: difference vector $(b,a,-1)$ in raw positions $m-2,m-1,m$ and zeros
 1004: elsewhere.  In the positive chamber place
 1005: $(b,b-a,1-a,1)$ in positions $m-3,\ldots,m$.  Each vector is the difference
 1006: of two digit blocks.  The recurrence shows that all $m-1$ consecutive output
 1007: ranks agree modulo $Q_m$, while the raw blocks differ.  For an invertible
 1008: aperture $m=2$, the two pairs $(0,0)$ and $(\kappa,d)$ have the same single
 1009: output, so again $\delta\ge m$.
 1010: 
 1011: The overlap graph is essential and irreducible because its underlying graph
 1012: is the full de Bruijn graph.  It is right-resolving: two edges with the same
 1013: initial vertex and different appended digits have rank difference
 1014: $hQ_{m-1}$ with $1\le|h|\le d$, which is nonzero modulo $Q_m$.  It is
 1015: left-resolving because two edges with the same terminal vertex and different
 1016: initial digits have nonzero rank difference of modulus at most $d<Q_m$.
 1017: By $\delta=m$, every output word of length $m$ labels a unique path and is
 1018: intrinsically synchronizing.  Distinct vertices have distinct follower
 1019: languages: a common right-infinite label would be decoded by
 1020: Theorem~\ref{thm:quadratic-optimal-causal-inverse} when $m\ge3$, or by
 1021: \eqref{eq:m2-central-decoder}--\eqref{eq:m2-right-decoder} when $m=2$, and
 1022: would force the two raw vertices to coincide.  The Fischer-cover criterion
 1023: now proves the cover assertion and its state count.
 1024: 
 1025: The gluing proof in Theorem~\ref{thm:full-quadratic-pisot-threshold} defines
 1026: $Y_{\beta,m}$ by forbidden output words of length at most $m+1$, so it is an
 1027: $m$-step SFT.  The difference certificates above give a length-$(m-1)$
 1028: output word which labels two paths ending at distinct Fischer states; for
 1029: $m=2$ the same role is played by the common label $(0,0)$.  Since the states
 1030: have distinct follower languages, this word is not intrinsically
 1031: synchronizing.  Every word of length $r$ in an $r$-step SFT is intrinsically
 1032: synchronizing.  Hence $Y_{\beta,m}$ is not an $(m-1)$-step SFT.
 1033: \end{proof}

    1: \section{Higher-degree simple-Parry causal obstructions}
    2: \label{sec:simple-parry-causal-obstructions}
    3: 
    4: The equalities between bounded-multiple order and optimal causal inverse
    5: length proved in Theorem~\ref{thm:quadratic-optimal-causal-inverse} do not
    6: extend universally beyond the stated quadratic cases.  We now identify the
    7: finite object that governs
    8: causal inversion in arbitrary simple-Parry rank folds and exhibit
    9: higher-degree counterexamples in both directions.  This section continues to
   10: concern the cyclic language rank of this article, not equality of numerical
   11: $\beta$-values.
   12: 
   13: Recall that Pisot bases have eventually periodic greedy expansions of one
   14: \cite{Schmidt1980}, whereas the finite expansion required below is the
   15: simple-Parry hypothesis.  The collision results through
   16: Theorem~\ref{thm:simple-parry-aperture-two} do not require the base to be
   17: Pisot.  Parry admissibility and the associated Bertrand numeration are standard
   18: \cite{Parry1960,CharlierCisterninoStipulanti2022}; the expansions of one for
   19: cubic Pisot numbers were classified by Bassino \cite{Bassino2002}.  We record
   20: the data needed
   21: to distinguish these published inputs from the new sliding-congruence
   22: statement.
   23: 
   24: Let $\beta\notin\mathbb Z$ be a simple-Parry number with
   25: \[
   26: d_\beta(1)=t_1t_2\cdots t_p0^\infty,
   27: \qquad t_1=d=\lfloor\beta\rfloor,
   28: \qquad t_p>0,
   29: \]
   30: where $p\ge2$ and $0\le t_i\le d$.  Put
   31: \[
   32: P_\beta(z)=z^p-t_1z^{p-1}-\cdots-t_p,
   33: \qquad
   34: \mathcal A_d=\{0,1,\ldots,d\},
   35: \qquad
   36: \Delta_d=[-d,d]\cap\mathbb Z.
   37: \]
   38: The raw system is the full shift $\mathcal A_d^{\mathbb Z}$, so $\Delta_d$
   39: is exactly its digit-difference alphabet.  Let $Q_n$ be the number of legal
   40: length-$n$ words.  In the low-to-high convention used throughout this
   41: article, the established Parry--Bertrand rank data are
   42: \begin{align}
   43: Q_0&=1,\nonumber\\
   44: Q_n&=1+\sum_{i=1}^n t_iQ_{n-i} &&(1\le n<p),\label{eq:simple-parry-Q}\\
   45: Q_n&=\sum_{i=1}^p t_iQ_{n-i} &&(n\ge p),\nonumber
   46: \end{align}
   47: and
   48: \[
   49: \Rank_{\beta,m}(w)=\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}w_jQ_j
   50: \]
   51: maps the legal length-$m$ words, in colexicographic order, bijectively onto
   52: $\{0,\ldots,Q_m-1\}$.  We therefore define $\Fold_{\beta,m}$ and
   53: $\Phi_{\beta,m}$ by the same rank reduction modulo $Q_m$ as in
   54: Definition~\ref{def:quadratic-parry-fold}.
   55: 
   56: For $m\ge2$, define
   57: \begin{equation}
   58: \ell_{\rm cau}(\beta,m)
   59: =\min\{L\ge1:a_t=\psi(y_t,\ldots,y_{t+L-1})
   60: \text{ for every }y=\Phi_{\beta,m}(a)\},
   61: \label{eq:simple-parry-causal-length}
   62: \end{equation}
   63: with value $\infty$ if no such $L$ exists.  Also set
   64: \[
   65: \mathfrak a(P_\beta,d)
   66: =\min\{\deg H:0\ne H\in(P_\beta),\ H(0)\ne0,
   67: \ |[z^j]H|\le d\text{ for all }j\}.
   68: \]
   69: 
   70: \begin{proposition}[Degeneration of the bounded-multiple order]
   71: \label{prop:simple-parry-bounded-order}
   72: Under the preceding simple-Parry hypotheses,
   73: \[
   74: \mathfrak a(P_\beta,d)=p.
   75: \]
   76: \end{proposition}
   77: 
   78: \begin{proof}
   79: The coefficient vector of $P_\beta$ consists of the leading coefficient
   80: $1$ and the coefficients $-t_1,\ldots,-t_p$.  It lies in $\Delta_d$, and its
   81: constant coefficient $-t_p$ is nonzero.  Hence
   82: $\mathfrak a(P_\beta,d)\le p$.  Conversely, every nonzero member of the
   83: principal ideal $(P_\beta)\subset\mathbb Z[z]$ has degree at least
   84: $\deg P_\beta=p$.  This proves equality.
   85: \end{proof}
   86: 
   87: For $L\ge1$, introduce the bounded sliding-congruence obstruction
   88: \begin{equation}
   89: \mathcal N_{\beta,m,L}
   90: =\left\{e\in\Delta_d^{m+L-1}:
   91: e_0\ne0,\quad
   92: \sum_{j=0}^{m-1}Q_je_{t+j}\equiv0\pmod{Q_m}
   93: \ (0\le t<L)\right\}.
   94: \label{eq:bad-sliding-congruence-set}
   95: \end{equation}
   96: Equivalently, let $T_{m,L}$ be the $L$-row banded Toeplitz matrix obtained by
   97: placing the kernel $(Q_0,\ldots,Q_{m-1})$ at offsets
   98: $t=0,\ldots,L-1$.  Then the last condition is
   99: $T_{m,L}e\equiv0\pmod{Q_m}$.
  100: 
  101: \begin{theorem}[Exact causal collision criterion]
  102: \label{thm:simple-parry-causal-collision}
  103: For every nonintegral simple-Parry number as above and every $m\ge2$,
  104: \begin{equation}
  105: \ell_{\rm cau}(\beta,m)
  106: =\min\{L\ge1:\mathcal N_{\beta,m,L}=\varnothing\},
  107: \label{eq:exact-causal-obstruction}
  108: \end{equation}
  109: where the minimum is $\infty$ when all the obstruction sets are nonempty.
  110: 
  111: More explicitly, let $\Gamma_{\beta,m}$ be the directed graph with vertex set
  112: $V=\Delta_d^{m-1}$.  There is an edge from
  113: $v=(v_0,\ldots,v_{m-2})$ to $w=(w_0,\ldots,w_{m-2})$ precisely when
  114: \[
  115: w_i=v_{i+1}\quad(0\le i<m-2)
  116: \]
  117: and
  118: \begin{equation}
  119: \sum_{j=0}^{m-2}Q_jv_j+Q_{m-1}w_{m-2}
  120: \equiv0\pmod{Q_m}.
  121: \label{eq:collision-graph-edge}
  122: \end{equation}
  123: If $B=\{v\in V:v_0\ne0\}$, then
  124: \begin{equation}
  125: \ell_{\rm cau}(\beta,m)
  126: =1+\sup\{\text{number of edges in a directed path starting in }B\}.
  127: \label{eq:collision-graph-longest-path}
  128: \end{equation}
  129: The supremum is infinite exactly when a directed cycle is reachable from
  130: $B$.  Furthermore,
  131: \begin{equation}
  132: \Phi_{\beta,m}\text{ is injective}
  133: \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
  134: \Gamma_{\beta,m}\text{ has no bi-infinite path other than the zero path}.
  135: \label{eq:collision-graph-injectivity}
  136: \end{equation}
  137: If $\Phi_{\beta,m}$ is injective, then its causal inverse is finite exactly
  138: when the zero vertex is not reachable from $B$.
  139: \end{theorem}
  140: 
  141: \begin{proof}
  142: Let $u,v\in\mathcal A_d^{m+L-1}$ and put $e_i=u_i-v_i$.  Since the legal
  143: rank is a bijection, the $t$th output symbols of the two blocks are equal if
  144: and only if their raw ranks agree modulo $Q_m$, namely if and only if
  145: \[
  146: \sum_{j=0}^{m-1}Q_je_{t+j}\equiv0\pmod{Q_m}.
  147: \]
  148: Moreover, $u_0\ne v_0$ if and only if $e_0\ne0$.  Thus every pair of raw
  149: blocks whose first symbols remain ambiguous after $L$ outputs determines an
  150: element of $\mathcal N_{\beta,m,L}$.
  151: 
  152: Conversely, for any $e\in\Delta_d^{m+L-1}$ define coordinatewise
  153: \[
  154: u_i=\max\{e_i,0\},\qquad v_i=\max\{-e_i,0\}.
  155: \]
  156: Then $u_i,v_i\in\mathcal A_d$, $u_i-v_i=e_i$, and both finite blocks extend
  157: arbitrarily to points of the full shift.  Hence every element of
  158: $\mathcal N_{\beta,m,L}$ is realized by an actual output collision with
  159: distinct first symbols.  If the obstruction set is empty, the first raw
  160: symbol is constant on every fiber over an occurring length-$L$ output word;
  161: the resulting function on this finite output language is the decoder $\psi$
  162: in \eqref{eq:simple-parry-causal-length}.  This proves
  163: \eqref{eq:exact-causal-obstruction}.
  164: 
  165: A length-$L$ path in $\Gamma_{\beta,m}$ beginning at
  166: $(e_0,\ldots,e_{m-2})\in B$ is exactly a vector
  167: $e\in\mathcal N_{\beta,m,L}$: each edge appends one coordinate and imposes
  168: one of the $L$ congruences.  Formula
  169: \eqref{eq:collision-graph-longest-path} follows.  Since the graph is finite,
  170: arbitrarily long such paths exist exactly when a directed cycle is reachable
  171: from $B$.
  172: 
  173: A bi-infinite difference sequence labels a bi-infinite path in the graph if
  174: and only if all corresponding output ranks agree.  Every coordinate of that
  175: difference sequence is realized by two raw digits by the positive--negative
  176: part construction above.  Therefore a nonzero bi-infinite graph path is
  177: equivalent to a pair of distinct points with the same image, proving
  178: \eqref{eq:collision-graph-injectivity}.
  179: 
  180: Finally assume injectivity.  No directed cycle containing a nonzero vertex
  181: can then occur, because its periodic repetition would be a nonzero
  182: bi-infinite path.  The zero loop is consequently the only cycle reachable
  183: from $B$ that is compatible with injectivity.  Thus paths from $B$ have
  184: unbounded length exactly when they reach the zero vertex.  Combining this
  185: with \eqref{eq:collision-graph-longest-path} proves the last assertion.
  186: \end{proof}
  187: 
  188: \begin{corollary}[Causal completeness and periodic detection]
  189: \label{cor:simple-parry-causal-completeness}
  190: Under the preceding hypotheses, for every $m\ge2$ the following are
  191: equivalent:
  192: \begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)}]
  193:   \item $\Phi_{\beta,m}$ is injective;
  194:   \item $\ell_{\rm cau}(\beta,m)<\infty$;
  195:   \item no directed cycle of $\Gamma_{\beta,m}$ is reachable from $B$.
  196: \end{enumerate}
  197: If these conditions hold, then
  198: \begin{equation}
  199: 1\le \ell_{\rm cau}(\beta,m)
  200: \le (2d+1)^{m-1}-1.
  201: \label{eq:simple-parry-causal-state-bound}
  202: \end{equation}
  203: If they fail, there are distinct periodic raw points with the same image and
  204: with common period at most $(2d+1)^{m-1}-1$.  Thus injectivity in this class
  205: can be decided by a finite forward cycle search, and every failure has a
  206: bounded periodic certificate.
  207: \end{corollary}
  208: 
  209: \begin{proof}
  210: The recurrence \eqref{eq:simple-parry-Q} gives $Q_1=d+1$ and shows
  211: inductively that $Q_n>Q_{n-1}$: before the terminal index there is the
  212: additional summand $1$, while at and after that index the positive term
  213: $t_pQ_{n-p}$ accompanies $dQ_{n-1}$.  In particular, $Q_m>d$.
  214: 
  215: Let $\mathbf0=(0,\ldots,0)\in\Delta_d^{m-1}$.  If an edge
  216: $v\to\mathbf0$ exists, the overlap conditions give
  217: $v_1=\cdots=v_{m-2}=0$, and the edge congruence reduces to
  218: $v_0\equiv0\pmod{Q_m}$.  Since $|v_0|\le d<Q_m$, necessarily
  219: $v=\mathbf0$.  Hence the zero vertex has the zero vertex as its unique
  220: predecessor and is not reachable from $B$.
  221: 
  222: If $\Phi_{\beta,m}$ is injective, the last assertion of
  223: Theorem~\ref{thm:simple-parry-causal-collision} now gives a finite causal
  224: inverse.  The converse is immediate because a decoder recovers every raw
  225: coordinate.  A directed cycle reachable from $B$ cannot contain
  226: $\mathbf0$; repeating its first-coordinate labels gives a nonzero periodic
  227: difference sequence and hence, by taking positive and negative parts, two
  228: distinct periodic raw points with the same image.  Conversely, from any
  229: nonzero bi-infinite collision path, shift to a vertex in $B$.  Its forward
  230: path avoids $\mathbf0$ and therefore repeats among the
  231: $(2d+1)^{m-1}-1$ nonzero vertices, producing a reachable directed cycle.
  232: This proves all three equivalences and the periodic certificate, whose cycle
  233: may be chosen simple.
  234: 
  235: In the injective case the forward subgraph reachable from $B$ is acyclic and
  236: contains at most $(2d+1)^{m-1}-1$ vertices.  A directed path in it has at
  237: most $(2d+1)^{m-1}-2$ edges.  Formula
  238: \eqref{eq:collision-graph-longest-path} then gives
  239: \eqref{eq:simple-parry-causal-state-bound}.
  240: \end{proof}

  310: 
  311: \begin{proposition}[Pisot-free scope is strict]
  312: \label{prop:nonpisot-simple-parry-scope}
  313: Proposition~\ref{prop:simple-parry-bounded-order},
  314: Theorem~\ref{thm:simple-parry-causal-collision}, Corollary
  315: \ref{cor:simple-parry-causal-completeness}, and Theorem
  316: \ref{thm:simple-parry-aperture-two} hold for every nonintegral simple-Parry
  317: number; no Pisot hypothesis is required.  This strictly enlarges their scope.
  318: Indeed, let $\beta$ be the positive root of
  319: \begin{equation}
  320: x^4-2x^3-2=0.
  321: \label{eq:nonpisot-simple-parry-witness}
  322: \end{equation}
  323: Then
  324: \[
  325: d_\beta(1)=2002\,0^\infty,
  326: \]
  327: $\beta$ is not Pisot, and the aperture-two cyclic rank fold is injective with
  328: $\ell_{\rm cau}(\beta,2)=2$.
  329: \end{proposition}
  330: 
  331: \begin{proof}
  332: The recurrence for $Q_n$, the consecutive colex-rank formula, and Parry
  333: admissibility used above hold for every simple-Parry number
  334: \cite{Parry1960,CharlierCisterninoStipulanti2022}.  The proofs of the four
  335: listed results subsequently use only these data, the full raw alphabet
  336: $\{0,\ldots,d\}$, integer congruences modulo $Q_m$, and finite directed
  337: graphs.  No estimate on a Galois conjugate occurs.  This proves the claimed
  338: hypothesis removal.
  339: 
  340: It remains to show that the enlargement is strict.  The polynomial in
  341: \eqref{eq:nonpisot-simple-parry-witness} is Eisenstein at $2$, hence
  342: irreducible.  Its positive root satisfies
  343: \[
  344: 2<\beta<\frac{11}{5},
  345: \]
  346: because the polynomial takes the values $-2$ and $81/625$ at the two
  347: endpoints.  Its defining equation gives
  348: \[
  349: 1=2\beta^{-1}+2\beta^{-4}.
  350: \]
  351: The proper suffixes of $2002\,0^\infty$ are all lexicographically smaller
  352: than $2002\,0^\infty$: the first two begin with $0$, while the third is
  353: $2000^\infty<2002\,0^\infty$.  Parry's criterion therefore gives
  354: $d_\beta(1)=2002\,0^\infty$.
  355: 
  356: Write the negative real root as $-u$.  The function
  357: $g(u)=u^4+2u^3-2$ is strictly increasing for $u>0$, with $g(0)<0<g(1)$,
  358: so $0<u<1$.  The two remaining roots are a nonreal conjugate pair
  359: $z,\overline z$.  Moreover,
  360: \[
  361: \beta^4 g(2/\beta)
  362: =4(3+4\beta-\beta^3)>0,
  363: \]
  364: where the last inequality follows from
  365: $\beta<11/5$ and
  366: $3+4\beta-\beta^3>11-(11/5)^3=44/125$.
  367: Thus $u<2/\beta$.  The product of the four roots is $-2$, whence
  368: \[
  369: |z|^2=\frac{2}{\beta u}>1.
  370: \]
  371: Irreducibility makes $z$ a Galois conjugate of $\beta$, so $\beta$ is not
  372: Pisot.  Finally $d=2$, $p=4$, and
  373: $c_\beta=t_2+1=1<d$; case~\textup{(b)} of
  374: Theorem~\ref{thm:simple-parry-aperture-two} yields injectivity and
  375: $\ell_{\rm cau}(\beta,2)=2$.
  376: \end{proof}
  377: 
  378: The graph in Theorem~\ref{thm:simple-parry-causal-collision} is the arithmetic
  379: specialization of the standard fiber-product collision graph for a sliding
  380: block code; see \cite{LindMarcus1995,Nasu1995}.  Its substantive role here is
  381: that it separates the exact modulus-$Q_m$ survival depth from the unrelated
  382: degree of a bounded polynomial multiple.
  383: 
  384: We next show that, even in algebraic degree three, the causal lengths at
  385: injective apertures have no uniform bound.  Bassino's classification
  386: \cite{Bassino2002} contains the cubic Pisot family and its finite expansion
  387: of one used below.  We include a direct verification of those input data so
  388: that the new part of the argument is visibly confined to the sliding
  389: collision depth.
  390: 
  391: For $n\ge4$, put
  392: \begin{equation}
  393: M_n(x)=x^3-(n+2)x^2+2nx-n
  394: \label{eq:cubic-unbounded-polynomial}
  395: \end{equation}
  396: and define the word
  397: \begin{equation}
  398: w_n=n\,1\,2\cdots(n-2)\,n\,1\,0\,
  399: (n-1)(n-2)\cdots2\,0\,n.
  400: \label{eq:cubic-unbounded-parry-word}
  401: \end{equation}
  402: Thus $|w_n|=2n+2$.
  403: 
  404: \begin{lemma}[Cubic family data]
  405: \label{lem:cubic-unbounded-family-data}
  406: The polynomial $M_n$ has a unique real root $\beta_n$, this root belongs to
  407: $(n,n+1)$, and $\beta_n$ is a nonintegral cubic Pisot number.  Moreover,
  408: \[
  409: d_{\beta_n}(1)=w_n0^\infty.
  410: \]
  411: If $(Q_j)$ denotes its Parry--Bertrand language counts, then, for
  412: $0\le r\le n-1$,
  413: \begin{align}
  414: Q_0&=1,\qquad Q_1=n+1,\qquad Q_2=n^2+n+2,
  415: \label{eq:cubic-unbounded-initial-Q}\\
  416: Q_r&=(n+2)Q_{r-1}-2nQ_{r-2}+nQ_{r-3}
  417: \qquad(3\le r\le n-1).
  418: \label{eq:cubic-unbounded-Q-recurrence}
  419: \end{align}
  420: In particular $Q_{j+1}>nQ_j$ throughout this range.
  421: \end{lemma}
  422: 
  423: \begin{proof}
  424: One has
  425: \[
  426: M_n(n)=-n<0,
  427: \qquad
  428: M_n(n+1)=n^2-n-1>0.
  429: \]
  430: The identity
  431: \[
  432: M_n(x)=x^2(x-2)-n(x-1)^2
  433: \]
  434: shows that $M_n(x)<0$ for $x\le n$.  Indeed this is immediate for
  435: $x\le2$, while for $2<x\le n$ one uses
  436: \[
  437: \frac{x^2(x-2)}{(x-1)^2}
  438: =x-\frac{x}{(x-1)^2}<x\le n.
  439: \]
  440: If all three roots were real, every root other than the one in $(n,n+1)$
  441: would also have to exceed $n$; their sum could then not equal $n+2$.
  442: Consequently $\beta_n$ is the unique real root.  A reducible monic cubic
  443: over $\mathbb Z$ has an integral root, which is impossible because the only
  444: real root lies strictly between consecutive integers.  Thus $M_n$ is
  445: irreducible.  Its two remaining roots are conjugate, and their common
  446: modulus is
  447: \[
  448: \sqrt{\frac{n}{\beta_n}}<1
  449: \]
  450: by the product of the roots.  This proves the asserted Pisot and degree
  451: statements.
  452: 
  453: Let $P_{w_n}(x)=x^{2n+2}-\sum_{i=1}^{2n+2}(w_n)_i
  454: x^{2n+2-i}$ be the Parry polynomial of the displayed word.  Direct
  455: multiplication gives the polynomial identity
  456: \begin{equation}
  457: P_{w_n}(x)
  458: =M_n(x)\frac{(x^n-1)(x^{n+1}-1)}{(x-1)^2}.
  459: \label{eq:cubic-unbounded-parry-factorization}
  460: \end{equation}
  461: Hence $w_n$ represents one in base $\beta_n$.  The digit $n$ occurs only
  462: at the first, the $n$th, and the last positions.  The suffix beginning at
  463: the $n$th position starts with $n10$, whereas $w_n$ starts with $n12$;
  464: the last suffix is $n0^\infty$.  Every other proper suffix starts with a
  465: digit below $n$.  All proper suffixes are therefore lexicographically
  466: smaller than $w_n0^\infty$, so Parry's criterion gives the claimed greedy
  467: expansion.
  468: 
  469: For $r\le n-1$, its first $r$ digits are $t_1=n$ and $t_i=i-1$ for
  470: $2\le i\le r$.  Formula~\eqref{eq:simple-parry-Q} becomes
  471: \begin{equation}
  472: Q_r=1+nQ_{r-1}+\sum_{i=2}^r(i-1)Q_{r-i}.
  473: \label{eq:cubic-unbounded-positive-Q}
  474: \end{equation}
  475: This gives \eqref{eq:cubic-unbounded-initial-Q}, proves $Q_r>nQ_{r-1}$,
  476: and, by subtracting the formula at the preceding three indices, gives
  477: \eqref{eq:cubic-unbounded-Q-recurrence}.
  478: \end{proof}

  492: 
  493: \begin{lemma}[Parameter-uniform two-window carry exclusion]
  494: \label{lem:cubic-unbounded-two-window}
  495: Let $n\ge4$ and $2\le r\le n-1$.  If
  496: $e_0,\ldots,e_r\in[-n,n]\cap\mathbb Z$ and
  497: \[
  498: \sum_{j=0}^{r-1}Q_je_j=c_0Q_r,
  499: \qquad
  500: \sum_{j=0}^{r-1}Q_je_{j+1}=c_1Q_r
  501: \]
  502: for integers $c_0,c_1$, then $c_0,c_1\in\{-1,0,1\}$ and $c_1=0$.
  503: \end{lemma}
  504: 
  505: \begin{proof}
  506: Since $Q_{j+1}>nQ_j$, geometric summation gives
  507: \begin{equation}
  508: S_k<\frac{n}{n-1}Q_k.
  509: \label{eq:cubic-unbounded-geometric-bound}
  510: \end{equation}
  511: It follows that $nS_{r-1}<2Q_r$, so every carry is in
  512: $\{-1,0,1\}$.  We also record the possible last digit of one window.  If
  513: the carry is zero, \eqref{eq:cubic-unbounded-geometric-bound} gives
  514: $nS_{r-2}<2Q_{r-1}$, so the last digit belongs to $\{-1,0,1\}$.
  515: For a carry equal to one, the last digit is $n$.  To see this, note first
  516: that, for $r\ge3$,
  517: \[
  518: D_r+D_{r-1}>Q_{r-2}+3Q_{r-3}>(n+3)Q_{r-3}
  519: >nS_{r-3}.
  520: \]
  521: Together with $Q_{r-1}=nQ_{r-2}+D_{r-1}$, this says
  522: \begin{equation}
  523: Q_r-(n-1)Q_{r-1}>nS_{r-2}.
  524: \label{eq:cubic-unbounded-last-digit-gap}
  525: \end{equation}
  526: The case $r=2$ follows directly from
  527: $Q_2-(n-1)Q_1=n+3>n$.  Thus a last digit at most $n-1$ cannot produce
  528: the sum $Q_r$.  Sign symmetry gives last digit $-n$ for carry $-1$.
  529: 
  530: Suppose now, toward a contradiction, that $c_1=1$; the negative case is
  531: obtained by changing all signs.  Put $f_j=e_{j+1}$ for
  532: $0\le j\le r-2$.  The last-digit conclusion gives $e_r=n$, and hence
  533: \begin{equation}
  534: A:=\sum_{j=0}^{r-2}Q_jf_j=D_r.
  535: \label{eq:cubic-unbounded-prefix-D}
  536: \end{equation}
  537: Set $B=\sum_{j=0}^{r-2}Q_{j+1}f_j$.  We shall prove
  538: \begin{equation}
  539: n<B<Q_r-n.
  540: \label{eq:cubic-unbounded-shifted-gap}
  541: \end{equation}
  542: This is already explicit at the two small apertures.  If $r=2$, then
  543: $f_0=2$ and $B=2(n+1)$.  If $r=3$, equation
  544: $f_0+(n+1)f_1=n+4$ and the digit bounds give
  545: \[
  546: (f_0,f_1)=(3,1)\quad\hbox{or}\quad(2-n,2).
  547: \]
  548: The corresponding values of $B$ are $n^2+4n+5$ and $n^2+3n+6$.
  549: They satisfy \eqref{eq:cubic-unbounded-shifted-gap} for $n\ge4$.
  550:

  552: of $\beta=\beta_n$, and put
  553: \[
  554: s=\alpha+\overline\alpha=n+2-\beta,
  555: \qquad p=\alpha\overline\alpha=\frac n\beta.
  556: \]
  557: For $u_j=Q_{j+1}-\beta Q_j$, the cubic recurrence with its dominant
  558: $\beta$ component removed gives
  559: \[
  560: u_{j+2}=su_{j+1}-pu_j.
  561: \]
  562: The quadratic quantity
  563: \[
  564: I_j=u_{j+1}^2-su_ju_{j+1}+pu_j^2
  565: \]
  566: satisfies $I_j=p^jI_0$.  The two exact completed-square forms are
  567: \begin{align*}
  568: I_j&=\left(u_{j+1}-\frac{s}{2}u_j\right)^2
  569:  +\left(p-\frac{s^2}{4}\right)u_j^2,\\
  570: I_j&=p\left(u_j-\frac{s}{2p}u_{j+1}\right)^2
  571:  +\frac{p-s^2/4}{p}u_{j+1}^2.
  572: \end{align*}
  573: Since the conjugates are nonreal, $p-s^2/4>0$.  Finally, direct
  574: substitution of $M_n(\beta)=0$ gives
  575: \[
  576: 4\left(p-\frac{s^2}{4}\right)-I_0
  577: =\frac{(\beta-n)(2\beta+n-4)}{\beta}>0.
  578: \]
  579: Consequently $|u_j|<2$ throughout the required range, and
  580: \begin{equation}
  581: |B-\beta D_r|
  582: =\left|\sum_{j=0}^{r-2}u_jf_j\right|<2n(r-1).
  583: \label{eq:cubic-unbounded-conjugate-error}
  584: \end{equation}
  585: 
  586: The positive formula for $D_r$ gives $D_r>Q_{r-2}\ge Q_2$.  Since
  587: $r\le n-1$, the lower bound in
  588: \eqref{eq:cubic-unbounded-conjugate-error} is therefore larger than $n$.
  589: For the upper bound, \eqref{eq:cubic-unbounded-geometric-bound} and
  590: $Q_{r-3}>nQ_{r-4}$ give
  591: \[
  592: 2S_{r-4}<\frac{2}{n-1}Q_{r-3}
  593: \le(n-2)Q_{r-3}.
  594: \]
  595: Thus \eqref{eq:cubic-unbounded-D-alternate} yields
  596: $D_r<2Q_{r-2}<Q_{r-1}/2$.  Using $\beta-1<n$,
  597: \[
  598: Q_r-\beta D_r
  599: =nQ_{r-1}-(\beta-1)D_r
  600: >\frac n2Q_{r-1}.
  601: \]
  602: As $Q_{r-1}\ge Q_3=n^3+n^2+3n+4$, subtracting the error
  603: $2n(r-1)\le2n(n-2)$ still leaves more than $n$.  This proves
  604: \eqref{eq:cubic-unbounded-shifted-gap}.
  605: 
  606: The first window sum is $e_0+B$.  By
  607: \eqref{eq:cubic-unbounded-shifted-gap} and $|e_0|\le n$, it lies strictly
  608: between $0$ and $Q_r$, contradicting its membership in $Q_r\mathbb Z$.
  609: Therefore $c_1=0$.
  610: \end{proof}

  653: \]
  654: For two windows, Lemma~\ref{lem:cubic-unbounded-two-window} makes the
  655: second carry zero.  Then $e_1+(n+1)e_2=0$ forces $e_1=e_2=0$, and the
  656: first congruence forces $e_0=0$.  Hence
  657: $\mathcal K_{n,2,2}=\{0\}$.
  658: 
  659: The case $r=3$ can also be read without any omitted branch.  In a
  660: two-window path the second carry is zero, so
  661: \[
  662: e_1+(n+1)e_2+Q_2e_3=0.
  663: \]
  664: The last-digit bound in the preceding lemma gives
  665: $e_3\in\{-1,0,1\}$.  If $e_3=0$, division by $Q_1=n+1>n$ gives
  666: $e_1=e_2=0$; if $e_3=1$, it gives $(e_1,e_2)=(-2,-n)$; the remaining
  667: case is its negative.  The first congruence then determines
  668: $e_0=n-2$, $0$, or $2-n$, respectively.  Thus
  669: $\mathcal K_{n,3,2}=\{0,\pm E_{n,3}\}$.  Applying the already proved
  670: $r=2$ two-window classification to the last two zero-carry windows gives
  671: $\mathcal K_{n,3,3}=\{0\}$.
  672: 
  673: Now let $r\ge4$ and assume both assertions at aperture $r-1$.  Take
  674: $e\in\mathcal K_{n,r,r-1}$, and write its window sums as $c_tQ_r$.
  675: Repeated application of Lemma~\ref{lem:cubic-unbounded-two-window} gives
  676: $c_1=\cdots=c_{r-2}=0$.  Drop $e_0$ and the final coordinate of $e$.
  677: For $0\le t\le r-3$, the remaining length-$(r-1)$ window sum is
  678: \[
  679: \sum_{j=0}^{r-2}Q_je_{t+1+j}
  680: =-Q_{r-1}e_{t+r}.
  681: \]
  682: Hence the resulting vector belongs to
  683: $\mathcal K_{n,r-1,r-2}$ and is, by induction, $0$ or
  684: $\pm E_{n,r-1}$.  In the zero case the last zero-carry equation and then
  685: the first congruence force the two omitted coordinates to vanish.  In the
  686: nonzero cases the exact window sums already established force the final
  687: coordinate to be zero; the first congruence then forces the initial
  688: coordinate to be $-2$ or $2$.  These are precisely
  689: $E_{n,r}=(-2,E_{n,r-1},0)$ and its negative.  This proves the first
  690: classification.
  691: 
  692: Finally take $e\in\mathcal K_{n,r,r}$.  The last $r-1$ carries are zero.
  693: Dropping the first and final coordinates now produces an element of
  694: $\mathcal K_{n,r-1,r-1}$, which is zero by induction.  The last equation
  695: then kills the final coordinate, and the first equation kills the initial
  696: one.  Thus $\mathcal K_{n,r,r}=\{0\}$.
  697: \end{proof}
  698: 
  699: \begin{theorem}[Unbounded causal depth in algebraic degree three]
  700: \label{thm:cubic-unbounded-causal-depth}
  701: For every $n\ge4$, let $\beta_n\in(n,n+1)$ be the root of
  702: \eqref{eq:cubic-unbounded-polynomial} and put $m_n=n-1$.  Then $\beta_n$
  703: is a nonintegral cubic simple-Parry Pisot number, $\Phi_{\beta_n,m_n}$ is
  704: injective, and
  705: \begin{equation}
  706: \ell_{\rm cau}(\beta_n,m_n)=n-1.
  707: \label{eq:cubic-unbounded-causal-length}
  708: \end{equation}
  709: Consequently
  710: \[
  711: \sup\left\{\ell_{\rm cau}(\beta,m):
  712: \substack{\beta\text{ nonintegral cubic simple-Parry Pisot},\\
  713: m\ge2,\ \Phi_{\beta,m}\text{ injective}}\right\}=\infty.
  714: \]
  715: \end{theorem}
  716: 
  717: \begin{proof}
  718: Lemma~\ref{lem:cubic-unbounded-family-data} supplies all base and language
  719: hypotheses.  At $r=m_n$, Lemma
  720: \ref{lem:cubic-unbounded-terminal-paths} gives
  721: \[
  722: \mathcal N_{\beta_n,r,r-1}=\{E_{n,r},-E_{n,r}\},
  723: \qquad
  724: \mathcal N_{\beta_n,r,r}=\varnothing.
  725: \]
  726: The exact collision criterion, Theorem
  727: \ref{thm:simple-parry-causal-collision}, now gives
  728: $\ell_{\rm cau}(\beta_n,m_n)=m_n=n-1$.  Its finiteness implies
  729: injectivity by Corollary~\ref{cor:simple-parry-causal-completeness}, and
  730: letting $n$ tend to infinity proves the last assertion.
  731: \end{proof}

  101: \begin{theorem}[Exact causal collision criterion]
  102: \label{thm:simple-parry-causal-collision}
  103: For every nonintegral simple-Parry number as above and every $m\ge2$,
  104: \begin{equation}
  105: \ell_{\rm cau}(\beta,m)
  106: =\min\{L\ge1:\mathcal N_{\beta,m,L}=\varnothing\},
  107: \label{eq:exact-causal-obstruction}
  108: \end{equation}
  109: where the minimum is $\infty$ when all the obstruction sets are nonempty.
  110: 
  111: More explicitly, let $\Gamma_{\beta,m}$ be the directed graph with vertex set
  112: $V=\Delta_d^{m-1}$.  There is an edge from
  113: $v=(v_0,\ldots,v_{m-2})$ to $w=(w_0,\ldots,w_{m-2})$ precisely when
  114: \[
  115: w_i=v_{i+1}\quad(0\le i<m-2)
  116: \]
  117: and
  118: \begin{equation}
  119: \sum_{j=0}^{m-2}Q_jv_j+Q_{m-1}w_{m-2}
  120: \equiv0\pmod{Q_m}.
  121: \label{eq:collision-graph-edge}
  122: \end{equation}
  123: If $B=\{v\in V:v_0\ne0\}$, then
  124: \begin{equation}
  125: \ell_{\rm cau}(\beta,m)
  126: =1+\sup\{\text{number of edges in a directed path starting in }B\}.
  127: \label{eq:collision-graph-longest-path}
  128: \end{equation}
  129: The supremum is infinite exactly when a directed cycle is reachable from
  130: $B$.  Furthermore,
  131: \begin{equation}
  132: \Phi_{\beta,m}\text{ is injective}
  133: \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
  134: \Gamma_{\beta,m}\text{ has no bi-infinite path other than the zero path}.
  135: \label{eq:collision-graph-injectivity}
  136: \end{equation}
  137: If $\Phi_{\beta,m}$ is injective, then its causal inverse is finite exactly
  138: when the zero vertex is not reachable from $B$.
  139: \end{theorem}
  140: 
  141: \begin{proof}
  142: Let $u,v\in\mathcal A_d^{m+L-1}$ and put $e_i=u_i-v_i$.  Since the legal
  143: rank is a bijection, the $t$th output symbols of the two blocks are equal if
  144: and only if their raw ranks agree modulo $Q_m$, namely if and only if
  145: \[
  146: \sum_{j=0}^{m-1}Q_je_{t+j}\equiv0\pmod{Q_m}.
  147: \]
  148: Moreover, $u_0\ne v_0$ if and only if $e_0\ne0$.  Thus every pair of raw
  149: blocks whose first symbols remain ambiguous after $L$ outputs determines an
  150: element of $\mathcal N_{\beta,m,L}$.
  151: 
  152: Conversely, for any $e\in\Delta_d^{m+L-1}$ define coordinatewise
  153: \[
  154: u_i=\max\{e_i,0\},\qquad v_i=\max\{-e_i,0\}.
  155: \]
  156: Then $u_i,v_i\in\mathcal A_d$, $u_i-v_i=e_i$, and both finite blocks extend
  157: arbitrarily to points of the full shift.  Hence every element of
  158: $\mathcal N_{\beta,m,L}$ is realized by an actual output collision with
  159: distinct first symbols.  If the obstruction set is empty, the first raw
  160: symbol is constant on every fiber over an occurring length-$L$ output word;
  161: the resulting function on this finite output language is the decoder $\psi$
  162: in \eqref{eq:simple-parry-causal-length}.  This proves
  163: \eqref{eq:exact-causal-obstruction}.
  164: 
  165: A length-$L$ path in $\Gamma_{\beta,m}$ beginning at
  166: $(e_0,\ldots,e_{m-2})\in B$ is exactly a vector
  167: $e\in\mathcal N_{\beta,m,L}$: each edge appends one coordinate and imposes
  168: one of the $L$ congruences.  Formula
  169: \eqref{eq:collision-graph-longest-path} follows.  Since the graph is finite,
  170: arbitrarily long such paths exist exactly when a directed cycle is reachable
  171: from $B$.
  172: 
  173: A bi-infinite difference sequence labels a bi-infinite path in the graph if
  174: and only if all corresponding output ranks agree.  Every coordinate of that
  175: difference sequence is realized by two raw digits by the positive--negative
  176: part construction above.  Therefore a nonzero bi-infinite graph path is
  177: equivalent to a pair of distinct points with the same image, proving
  178: \eqref{eq:collision-graph-injectivity}.
  179: 
  180: Finally assume injectivity.  No directed cycle containing a nonzero vertex
  181: can then occur, because its periodic repetition would be a nonzero
  182: bi-infinite path.  The zero loop is consequently the only cycle reachable
  183: from $B$ that is compatible with injectivity.  Thus paths from $B$ have
  184: unbounded length exactly when they reach the zero vertex.  Combining this
  185: with \eqref{eq:collision-graph-longest-path} proves the last assertion.
  186: \end{proof}
  187: 
  188: \begin{corollary}[Causal completeness and periodic detection]
  189: \label{cor:simple-parry-causal-completeness}
  190: Under the preceding hypotheses, for every $m\ge2$ the following are
  191: equivalent:
  192: \begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)}]
  193:   \item $\Phi_{\beta,m}$ is injective;
  194:   \item $\ell_{\rm cau}(\beta,m)<\infty$;
  195:   \item no directed cycle of $\Gamma_{\beta,m}$ is reachable from $B$.
  196: \end{enumerate}
  197: If these conditions hold, then
  198: \begin{equation}
  199: 1\le \ell_{\rm cau}(\beta,m)
  200: \le (2d+1)^{m-1}-1.
  201: \label{eq:simple-parry-causal-state-bound}
  202: \end{equation}
  203: If they fail, there are distinct periodic raw points with the same image and
  204: with common period at most $(2d+1)^{m-1}-1$.  Thus injectivity in this class
  205: can be decided by a finite forward cycle search, and every failure has a
  206: bounded periodic certificate.
  207: \end{corollary}
  208: 
  209: \begin{proof}
  210: The recurrence \eqref{eq:simple-parry-Q} gives $Q_1=d+1$ and shows
  211: inductively that $Q_n>Q_{n-1}$: before the terminal index there is the
  212: additional summand $1$, while at and after that index the positive term
  213: $t_pQ_{n-p}$ accompanies $dQ_{n-1}$.  In particular, $Q_m>d$.
  214: 
  215: Let $\mathbf0=(0,\ldots,0)\in\Delta_d^{m-1}$.  If an edge
  216: $v\to\mathbf0$ exists, the overlap conditions give
  217: $v_1=\cdots=v_{m-2}=0$, and the edge congruence reduces to
  218: $v_0\equiv0\pmod{Q_m}$.  Since $|v_0|\le d<Q_m$, necessarily
  219: $v=\mathbf0$.  Hence the zero vertex has the zero vertex as its unique
  220: predecessor and is not reachable from $B$.
  221: 
  222: If $\Phi_{\beta,m}$ is injective, the last assertion of
  223: Theorem~\ref{thm:simple-parry-causal-collision} now gives a finite causal
  224: inverse.  The converse is immediate because a decoder recovers every raw
  225: coordinate.  A directed cycle reachable from $B$ cannot contain
  226: $\mathbf0$; repeating its first-coordinate labels gives a nonzero periodic
  227: difference sequence and hence, by taking positive and negative parts, two
  228: distinct periodic raw points with the same image.  Conversely, from any
  229: nonzero bi-infinite collision path, shift to a vertex in $B$.  Its forward
  230: path avoids $\mathbf0$ and therefore repeats among the
  231: $(2d+1)^{m-1}-1$ nonzero vertices, producing a reachable directed cycle.
  232: This proves all three equivalences and the periodic certificate, whose cycle
  233: may be chosen simple.
  234: 
  235: In the injective case the forward subgraph reachable from $B$ is acyclic and
  236: contains at most $(2d+1)^{m-1}-1$ vertices.  A directed path in it has at
  237: most $(2d+1)^{m-1}-2$ edges.  Formula
  238: \eqref{eq:collision-graph-longest-path} then gives
  239: \eqref{eq:simple-parry-causal-state-bound}.
  240: \end{proof}

I verified the bibliographic records rather than relying on shorthand: Amoroso–Patt and Richardson are both in JCSS 6 (1972), Nasu is in Mathematical Systems Theory 11 (1977), Head’s paper is “Injectivity From Unambiguity” in Complex Systems 3 (1989), and Ashley’s decoder-window papers are IEEE TIT 34 (1988) and 42 (1996). I’m adding these records, Bertrand–Mathis 1989, and the published 2018 Drungilas et al. citation now; this file will be compiled before any prose file is edited.
